# Ambiently homeomorphic isolated hypersurfaces of multiplicities two and three

<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a negative answer to the embedded Zariski multiplicity conjecture. We construct two reduced hypersurface germs that are ambiently homeomorphic but have multiplicities two and three. Both have isolated singularities and lie in a common complex affine space of dimension divisible by eight. Their defining function germs are also topologically right equivalent, and the same examples answer Arnold's corank problem negatively for ambient topological equivalence.

- **stmt-thm-main** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a finite integer $N>3$, divisible by eight, and there are reduced polynomials $f_1,f_2\in\mathbb R[z_1,\ldots,z_N]$, weighted homogeneous for positive rational weights, such that:* 1. *each $f_j$ has an isolated critical point at the origin;* 2. *$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0f_1=2$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0f_2=3$;* 3. *there is a homeomorphism germ of ambient pairs $$(\mathbb C^N,V(f_1),0)\ \cong\ (\mathbb C^N,V(f_2),0).$$
  - Explanation: Ambient topology does not determine multiplicity for these reduced isolated weighted homogeneous germs.
- **stmt-bg-milnor** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-milnor)): Let $s\geq2$, and let $f:(\mathbb C^s,0)\to(\mathbb C,0)$ be a holomorphic germ with an isolated critical point at $0$, with $f(0)=0$. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, the link $K_f=f^{-1}(0)\cap S^{2s-1}_\varepsilon$ is a smooth closed manifold of dimension $2s-3$, and $$f/\|f\|:S^{2s-1}_\varepsilon\setminus K_f\longrightarrow S^1$$ is a locally trivial fibration. Each fiber is the interior of its closure $F_f$, a compact manifold with boundary $K_f$. Both $F_f$ and its interior are homotopy equivalent to a finite bouquet of $(s-1)$-spheres. The link is $(s-3)$-connected.
  - Explanation: The isolated critical-point hypotheses provide a Milnor fibration and highly connected links.
- **stmt-bg-ts** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-ts)): Suppose $f$ and $g$ have isolated critical points in $a$ and $b$ variables, respectively. There is an isomorphism of integral lattices $$L_{f+g}\cong L_f\otimes_{\mathbb Z}L_g$$ under which $$M_{f+g}\cong M_f\otimes M_g, \qquad S_{f+g}\cong(-1)^{ab}S_f\otimes S_g.$$ The assertion includes one-variable powers, with reduced zeroth homology.
  - Explanation: Disjoint sums realize tensor products of lattices, monodromy, and signed Seifert forms.
- **stmt-bg-spectrum** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-spectrum)): If $f$ is weighted homogeneous of normalized degree $1$ and has an isolated critical point, its spectral polynomial is $$\label{eq:bg-spectrum} P_f(t)=\sum_{\beta\in\operatorname{Sp}^{+}(f)}t^\beta =\prod_{j=1}^s\frac{t^{w_j}-t}{1-t^{w_j}}.$$ Exponents are counted with multiplicity. Their classes modulo $\mathbb Z$ determine the eigenvalues of $M_f$ by exponentiation, with a fixed choice of sign in the exponent. The operator $M_f$ has finite order.
  - Explanation: Normalized weights determine the positive spectral polynomial and finite-order monodromy.
- **stmt-bg-real** (classification, [source](#stmt-bg-real)): Let $f$ and $g$ be isolated weighted homogeneous singularities in the same number of variables. If their multisets of positive spectral exponents agree modulo $2\mathbb Z$, their real Seifert forms are isomorphic.
  - Explanation: Spectra modulo the stated period determine real Seifert forms in a common dimension.
- **stmt-bg-orlik** (classification, [source](#stmt-bg-orlik)): Suppose $f$ is a sum of chains $$x_1^{a_1}x_2+\cdots+x_{r-1}^{a_{r-1}}x_r+x_r^{a_r}, \qquad a_i\geq2,$$ including chains with $r=1$. Write the characteristic polynomial of $M_f$ as $\prod_d\Phi_d(t)^{c_d}$, where $\Phi_d$ is cyclotomic. Then, as an integral module with operator $t=M_f$, $$\label{eq:bg-orlik} (L_f,M_f)\cong \bigoplus_{j=1}^{\max c_d}\mathbb Z[t]/\bigl(p_j(t)\bigr), \qquad p_j(t)=\prod_{c_d\geq j}\Phi_d(t).$$ In particular, the eigenvalues with multiplicity determine this integral monodromy module within the indicated class of singularities.
  - Explanation: Within sums of chains, eigenvalue multiplicities determine the integral monodromy module.
- **stmt-bg-hasse** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bg-hasse)): Two nondegenerate quadratic forms over a number field $F$ are isometric over $F$ if and only if they are isometric over $F_v$ for every place $v$, including the archimedean places.
  - Explanation: Isometry of quadratic forms is characterized by isometry at every completion.
- **stmt-bg-approximation** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-bg-approximation)): Let $G$ be a connected, simply connected, absolutely almost simple algebraic group over a number field $F$. If $\prod_{v\mid\infty}G(F_v)$ is noncompact, then $G(F)$ is dense in $G(\mathbb A_{F,f})$, the group of finite adelic points.
  - Explanation: Noncompactness at the archimedean places gives density in the finite adelic group.
- **stmt-bg-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-bg-classification)): A smooth closed manifold of dimension $d\geq5$ that is homotopy equivalent to $S^d$ is homeomorphic to $S^d$.* *Let $q\geq3$, and let $K_1,K_2\subset S^{2q+1}$ be smooth oriented submanifolds homeomorphic to $S^{2q-1}$. Suppose the knots are *simple*, meaning $$\pi_1(S^{2q+1}\setminus K_j)\cong\mathbb Z, \qquad \pi_i(S^{2q+1}\setminus K_j)=0\quad(2\leq i\leq q-1).$$ If their integral Seifert matrices are congruent, then they are ambiently isotopic. In particular their embedded sphere pairs are homeomorphic.
  - Explanation: The stated dimension, spherical, and simplicity hypotheses permit recognition and classification of the embedded links.
- **stmt-seed-product** (construction, [source](#stmt-seed-product)): Set $d=3^{11}$ and $$\mathcal B=\{b\in\mathbb Z:0<b<d/2,\ 3\nmid b\},\qquad A_b=\frac{d-b}{b},\qquad R_b=\frac{(2d+b)(2d-b)}{(3d-b)(d+b)}.$$ There are integers $e_b$, $b\in\mathcal B$, such that $$\label{seed:products} E:=\sum_{b\in\mathcal B}e_b>0, \qquad \prod_{b\in\mathcal B}A_b^{e_b} =\prod_{b\in\mathcal B}R_b^{e_b}=1.$$
  - Explanation: An integer valuation relation has positive coefficient sum and preserves both rational products.
- **stmt-seed-chain** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-seed-chain)): Let $D$ be a positive odd integer and let $c\in\mathbb Z$ satisfy $0<c<D/2$. There is a chain polynomial $$\label{seed:chain-polynomial} F_c=x_1^{a_1}x_2+\cdots+x_{\ell-1}^{a_{\ell-1}}x_\ell +x_\ell^{a_\ell}$$ of weighted degree $1$, whose first weight is $c/D$, all of whose weights are positive and less than $1/2$, and all of whose ordinary monomial degrees are at least $3$. It has an isolated critical point at the origin. Removing its first variable and first monomial gives a chain with exactly the remaining weights, when $\ell>1$.
  - Explanation: A decreasing-remainder construction completes a rational weight to an isolated chain.
- **stmt-seed-seeds** (construction, [source](#stmt-seed-seeds)): There are real-coefficient weighted homogeneous polynomials $p_1,p_2$, each in an even number $s_0$ of variables, such that:* 1. *both are sums, in disjoint variables, of chains with exponents at least $2$, and both have isolated critical points;* 2. *$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0p_1=2$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0p_2=3$;* 3. *their spectral multisets are equal modulo $2$;* 4. *their real Seifert forms are isomorphic, and, separately, their integral monodromy modules are isomorphic;* 5. *both monodromy operators have order dividing $D=3^{12}$.
  - Explanation: The seeds have unequal ordinary orders and separate real Seifert-form and integral monodromy-module identifications.
- **stmt-seed-prepared** (construction, [source](#stmt-seed-prepared)): In two new variables on each side, put $$\label{seed:q-definition} q_j=p_j+x^5+y^7,\qquad j=1,2, \qquad s=s_0+2.$$ These are real-coefficient isolated sums of chains in the same even number $s$ of variables, with orders $2$ and $3$. Their real Seifert forms are isomorphic, and their integral monodromy modules are isomorphic. Both monodromies have odd order dividing $$m=3^{12}\cdot5\cdot7,$$ and neither has eigenvalue $1$.
  - Explanation: Common pure powers preserve the ordinary orders and remove the fixed monodromy eigenvalue.
- **stmt-local-symmetrize** (construction, [source](#stmt-local-symmetrize)): Each $B_j$ is an integral unimodular symmetric form preserved by $T_j$. The pair $(B_j,T_j)$ recovers $S_j$ by $S_j=B_jT_j^{-r}$. The real Seifert isometry is an equivariant real isometry of the symmetric data. Symmetrization commutes with tensor products of even-variable factors, up to the common signs in Theorem <a href="#bg:ts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bg:ts">3</a>.
  - Explanation: Odd-order monodromy in even dimension permits reversible integral symmetrization.
- **stmt-local-lifting** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-local-lifting)): Let $\ell$ be an odd prime and let $\mathcal A$ be a commutative $\mathbb Z_\ell$-algebra that is finite as a $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module. Write $J$ for its Jacobson radical. Then $\mathcal A/J$ is a product of finite fields, and $\mathcal A$ is complete for the $J$-adic topology. For a unit $u\in\mathcal A$:* 1. *if its image in every residue field is a square, then $u$ is a square in $\mathcal A$;* 2. *$u=a^2+b^2$ for some $a,b\in\mathcal A$.
  - Explanation: In a finite commutative operator algebra at an odd prime, residue square roots lift and units are sums of squares.
- **stmt-local-odd-primes** (theorem, [source](#stmt-local-odd-primes)): Let $L$ be a finite free $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module, with $\ell$ odd, and let $T$ be an automorphism of $L$. If $B_1,B_2$ are unimodular symmetric forms preserved by $T$, then $$(L\otimes L,T\otimes T,B_1\otimes B_1) \cong (L\otimes L,T\otimes T,B_2\otimes B_2)$$ by an equivariant integral isometry.
  - Explanation: Tensor squares of invariant unimodular symmetric forms on a common operator module are equivariantly isometric at odd primes.
- **stmt-local-dyadic** (classification, [source](#stmt-local-dyadic)): Let $L$ be a finite free $\mathbb Z_2$-module, and let $T$ satisfy $T^m=1$ for an odd $m$. Suppose $1$ is not an eigenvalue of $T$ on $L\otimes\mathbb Q_2$. Any two $T$-invariant unimodular symmetric forms on $L$ are equivariantly isometric over $\mathbb Z_2$.
  - Explanation: Odd-order monodromy without a fixed eigenvalue gives dyadic uniqueness of invariant unimodular symmetric forms.
- **stmt-local-hermitian-lengths** (classification, [source](#stmt-local-hermitian-lengths)): Let $E/F$ be a quadratic field extension with $\operatorname{char}F\ne2$, and let $h_1,h_2$ be nonsingular hermitian forms. If the quadratic forms $$Q_{h_j}(x)=h_j(x,x)$$ on the underlying $F$-spaces are isometric, then $h_1,h_2$ are $E$-linearly isometric.
  - Explanation: Quadratic length forms determine nonsingular hermitian forms over the specified quadratic extension.
- **stmt-local-hasse-descent** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-local-hasse-descent)): Let $(V_j,U_j,\beta_j)$ be rational vector spaces with nonsingular symmetric forms preserved by operators of finite odd order. If they are equivariantly isometric over $\mathbb R$ and over $\mathbb Q_\ell$ for every prime $\ell$, then they are equivariantly isometric over $\mathbb Q$.
  - Explanation: Equivariant isometries over every rational completion and the real numbers descend to a rational isometry.
- **stmt-local-reverser** (construction, [source](#stmt-local-reverser)): There is an integral involution $C_j$ of $L_j$ such that $$C_j^{-1}T_jC_j=T_j^{-1},\qquad C_j^{\mathsf t}B_jC_j=B_j.$$
  - Explanation: Real coefficients provide an integral form-preserving involution reversing monodromy.
- **stmt-global-determinants** (theorem, [source](#stmt-global-determinants)): For every prime $\ell$, the ratio $\widehat R^{-1}\widehat D_\ell$ has determinant $1$ on every eigenspace of $\tau_1\otimes\tau_1$ over $\overline{\mathbb Q_\ell}$. This remains true after tensoring both maps with identity maps on any common finite-order operator data.
  - Explanation: A reverser-twisted tensor square cancels all eigenspace determinant discrepancies.
- **stmt-global-indefinite** (bound, [source](#stmt-global-indefinite)): Every eigenvalue of $\Theta_j$ has odd order divisible by both $11$ and $13$. On every complex eigenspace, the hermitian form $$(x,y)\longmapsto\beta_j(x,\overline y)$$ is nondegenerate and indefinite; the complex dimension of that eigenspace is at least $21$.
  - Explanation: The added common factors exclude real eigenvalues and make every eigenspace hermitian form indefinite.
- **stmt-global-integral** (construction, [source](#stmt-global-integral)): The triples $H_1$ and $H_2$ in <a href="#global:final-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:final-data">[global:final-data]</a> are isometric over $\mathbb Z$: there is a lattice isomorphism $U:\Lambda_1\longrightarrow\Lambda_2$ satisfying $$U\Theta_1=\Theta_2U, \qquad U^{\mathsf t}\beta_2U=\beta_1.$$
  - Explanation: Strong approximation turns the rational and local maps into an integral equivariant isometry.
- **stmt-topo-analytic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-topo-analytic)): The polynomials $f_1,f_2$ have isolated critical points at the origin, are reduced both as polynomials and as analytic germs, and satisfy $\{\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0(f_1),\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0(f_2)\}=\{2,3\}$.
  - Explanation: Disjoint sums preserve isolated critical points, reducedness, and the unequal ordinary orders.
- **stmt-topo-sphere** (theorem, [source](#stmt-topo-sphere)): Each $K_j$ is a homotopy sphere, and each embedding $K_j\subset S^{2n+1}_\varepsilon$ is a simple knot.
  - Explanation: The final links are homotopy spheres and satisfy the simple-knot conditions.
- **stmt-topo-arnold-corank** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-topo-arnold-corank)): In the finite ambient dimension $N>3$ divisible by eight in <a href="#topo:dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topo:dimension">[topo:dimension]</a>, the polynomials $f_1,f_2$ in <a href="#topo:polynomials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="topo:polynomials">[topo:polynomials]</a> have different Hessian coranks, although their reduced complex hypersurface germs are ambiently homeomorphic. Both polynomials have real coefficients, are weighted homogeneous with positive rational weights, and have isolated critical points at the origin.
  - Explanation: Ambient topological equivalence does not preserve Hessian corank for the constructed pair.
- **stmt-topo-right-equivalence** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-topo-right-equivalence)): For the same polynomials $f_1,f_2$ and the single finite ambient dimension $N>3$ divisible by eight in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, there is a homeomorphism germ $$\varphi:(\mathbb C^N,0)\longrightarrow(\mathbb C^N,0) \qquad\text{such that}\qquad f_1=f_2\circ\varphi.$$ Nevertheless, their multiplicities are respectively $2$ and $3$. Thus neither multiplicity nor multiplicity modulo two is invariant under topological right equivalence, even for reduced real-coefficient weighted homogeneous holomorphic germs with positive rational weights and isolated critical points at the origin.
  - Explanation: Topological right equivalence preserves neither multiplicity nor its parity for this pair.
- **stmt-topo-tangent-milnor** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-topo-tangent-milnor)): In the single ambient dimension $N+1$, put $$\widetilde f_j(z,t)=f_j(z)+t^2,\qquad j=1,2.$$ The germs $\widetilde f_1,\widetilde f_2:(\mathbb C^{N+1},0)\to(\mathbb C,0)$ are topologically right equivalent and have isolated critical points at the origin, but $$H_0\bigl((\widetilde f_1)_*^{-1}(1);\mathbb Z\bigr)\cong\mathbb Z, \qquad H_0\bigl((\widetilde f_2)_*^{-1}(1);\mathbb Z\bigr)\cong\mathbb Z^2.$$ Thus integral homology of the initial-form Milnor fibre is not invariant under topological right equivalence, already in degree zero. In particular, the initial-form Milnor-fibre homotopy conjecture of Budur, Fernández de Bobadilla, Lê, and Nguyen (Budur et al. 2022, Conjecture 1.7 in arXiv v3) [[BudurEtAl2022ContactLoci](#ref-BudurEtAl2022ContactLoci)] fails.
  - Explanation: Common quadratic stabilization preserves right equivalence while the initial-form unit fibres have different zeroth homology.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-bg-approximation",
        "exact_quote": "Let $G$ be a connected, simply connected, absolutely almost simple algebraic group over a number field $F$. If $\\prod_{v\\mid\\infty}G(F_v)$ is noncompact, then $G(F)$ is dense in $G(\\mathbb A_{F,f})$, the group of finite adelic points.",

        "anchor": "stmt-bg-classification",
        "exact_quote": "A smooth closed manifold of dimension $d\\geq5$ that is homotopy equivalent to $S^d$ is homeomorphic to $S^d$.*\n\n*Let $q\\geq3$, and let $K_1,K_2\\subset S^{2q+1}$ be smooth oriented submanifolds homeomorphic to $S^{2q-1}$. Suppose the knots are *simple*, meaning $$\\pi_1(S^{2q+1}\\setminus K_j)\\cong\\mathbb Z,\n \\qquad\n \\pi_i(S^{2q+1}\\setminus K_j)=0\\quad(2\\leq i\\leq q-1).$$ If their integral Seifert matrices are congruent, then they are ambiently isotopic. In particular their embedded sphere pairs are homeomorphic.",
        "source_id": "PM-059.1"
      },

        "exact_quote": "Set $d=3^{11}$ and $$\\mathcal B=\\{b\\in\\mathbb Z:0<b<d/2,\\ 3\\nmid b\\},\\qquad\n A_b=\\frac{d-b}{b},\\qquad\n R_b=\\frac{(2d+b)(2d-b)}{(3d-b)(d+b)}.$$ There are integers $e_b$, $b\\in\\mathcal B$, such that $$\\label{seed:products}\n E:=\\sum_{b\\in\\mathcal B}e_b>0,\n \\qquad \\prod_{b\\in\\mathcal B}A_b^{e_b}\n =\\prod_{b\\in\\mathcal B}R_b^{e_b}=1.$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-seed-chain",
        "exact_quote": "Let $D$ be a positive odd integer and let $c\\in\\mathbb Z$ satisfy $0<c<D/2$. There is a chain polynomial $$\\label{seed:chain-polynomial}\n F_c=x_1^{a_1}x_2+\\cdots+x_{\\ell-1}^{a_{\\ell-1}}x_\\ell\n       +x_\\ell^{a_\\ell}$$ of weighted degree $1$, whose first weight is $c/D$, all of whose weights are positive and less than $1/2$, and all of whose ordinary monomial degrees are at least $3$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-seed-seeds",
        "exact_quote": "There are real-coefficient weighted homogeneous polynomials $p_1,p_2$, each in an even number $s_0$ of variables, such that:*\n\n1.  *both are sums, in disjoint variables, of chains with exponents at least $2$, and both have isolated critical points;*\n\n2.  *$\\mathop{\\mathrm{ord}}_0p_1=2$ and $\\mathop{\\mathrm{ord}}_0p_2=3$;*\n\n3.  *their spectral multisets are equal modulo $2$;*\n\n4.  *their real Seifert forms are isomorphic, and, separately, their integral monodromy modules are isomorphic;*\n\n5.  *both monodromy operators have order dividing $D=3^{12}$.",
        "source_id": "PM-059.1"
      },

        "exact_quote": "In two new variables on each side, put $$\\label{seed:q-definition}\n q_j=p_j+x^5+y^7,\\qquad j=1,2,\n \\qquad s=s_0+2.$$ These are real-coefficient isolated sums of chains in the same even number $s$ of variables, with orders $2$ and $3$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-local-symmetrize",
        "exact_quote": "Each $B_j$ is an integral unimodular symmetric form preserved by $T_j$. The pair $(B_j,T_j)$ recovers $S_j$ by $S_j=B_jT_j^{-r}$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-local-lifting",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\ell$ be an odd prime and let $\\mathcal A$ be a commutative $\\mathbb Z_\\ell$-algebra that is finite as a $\\mathbb Z_\\ell$-module. Write $J$ for its Jacobson radical. Then $\\mathcal A/J$ is a product of finite fields, and $\\mathcal A$ is complete for the $J$-adic topology. For a unit $u\\in\\mathcal A$:*\n\n1.  *if its image in every residue field is a square, then $u$ is a square in $\\mathcal A$;*\n\n2.  *$u=a^2+b^2$ for some $a,b\\in\\mathcal A$.",
        "source_id": "PM-059.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-local-lifting",
          "exact_quote": "Let $\\ell$ be an odd prime and let $\\mathcal A$ be a commutative $\\mathbb Z_\\ell$-algebra that is finite as a $\\mathbb Z_\\ell$-module. Write $J$ for its Jacobson radical. Then $\\mathcal A/J$ is a product of finite fields, and $\\mathcal A$ is complete for the $J$-adic topology. For a unit $u\\in\\mathcal A$:*\n\n1.  *if its image in every residue field is a square, then $u$ is a square in $\\mathcal A$;*\n\n2.  *$u=a^2+b^2$ for some $a,b\\in\\mathcal A$.",
          "source_id": "PM-059.1"

      "exact_statement": "Let $\\ell$ be an odd prime and let $\\mathcal A$ be a commutative $\\mathbb Z_\\ell$-algebra that is finite as a $\\mathbb Z_\\ell$-module.         "anchor": "stmt-topo-tangent-milnor",
        "exact_quote": "In the single ambient dimension $N+1$, put $$\\widetilde f_j(z,t)=f_j(z)+t^2,\\qquad j=1,2.$$ The germs $\\widetilde f_1,\\widetilde f_2:(\\mathbb C^{N+1},0)\\to(\\mathbb C,0)$ are topologically right equivalent and have isolated critical points at the origin, but $$H_0\\bigl((\\widetilde f_1)_*^{-1}(1);\\mathbb Z\\bigr)\\cong\\mathbb Z,\n \\qquad\n H_0\\bigl((\\widetilde f_2)_*^{-1}(1);\\mathbb Z\\bigr)\\cong\\mathbb Z^2.$$ Thus integral homology of the initial-form Milnor fibre is not invariant under topological right equivalence, already in degree zero. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **ordinary order** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a nonzero convergent power series $f\in\mathbb C\{z_1,\ldots,z_N\}$ with $f(0)=0$, the order $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0f$ is the least total degree of a monomial with nonzero coefficient.
- **hypersurface multiplicity** ([source](#sec:introduction)): When $f$ is reduced, this integer is the multiplicity of the hypersurface germ $V(f)=\{f=0\}$.
- **ambient homeomorphism** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Two such germs are *ambiently homeomorphic* if a homeomorphism between neighborhoods of the origin in $\mathbb C^N$ fixes the origin and carries one hypersurface to the other.
- **positive rational weighted homogeneity** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A polynomial is *weighted homogeneous with positive rational weights* if there are positive integers $a_1,\ldots,a_N,d$ such that $f(t^{a_1}z_1,\ldots,t^{a_N}z_N)=t^d f(z)$; the normalized weights are $w_i=a_i/d$.
- **topological right equivalence** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Holomorphic function germs $f,g:(\mathbb C^N,0)\to(\mathbb C,0)$ are *topologically right equivalent* if $f=g\circ\varphi$ for a homeomorphism germ $\varphi:(\mathbb C^N,0)\to(\mathbb C^N,0)$.
- **Milnor lattice** ([source](#eq:bg-monodromy)): The free group $L_f=\widetilde H_{s-1}(F_f;\mathbb Z)$ is the *Milnor lattice*; for a one-variable power we use the reduced zeroth homology of its finite Milnor fiber.
- **integral Seifert form** ([source](#eq:bg-monodromy)): The unimodular integral Seifert form is $S_f(x,y)=\operatorname{lk}(x,y^+)$, where the second cycle is pushed positively to a neighboring page.
- **lattice and unimodularity** ([source](#pm-h-9)): A lattice is a finite-rank free abelian group; a bilinear form is unimodular if its determinant is $\pm1$.
- **equivariant isometry** ([source](#local:section)): Throughout, an *equivariant isometry* between data $(L,T,B)$ means an isometry of the forms that intertwines the displayed operators.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q-d | finite relation denominator | = | $3^{11}$ | The finite relation parameter. |
| Q-chain-weight | chain weights | < | $1/2$ | Positive odd denominator and the stated initial numerator. |
| Q-chain-degree | ordinary monomial degrees | ≥ | $3$ | The constructed nonterminal and terminal monomials. |
| Q-D | seed monodromy order divisor | = | $3^{12}$ | The common exponent bounding both operator orders. |
| Q-m | prepared monodromy order divisor | = | $3^{12}\cdot5\cdot7$ | The common odd exponent after adjoining pure powers. |
| Q-eigenspace | complex eigenspace dimension | ≥ | $21$ | Every eigenspace after the common factors are adjoined. |
| Q-N | ambient dimension | = | $4s_0+16$ | The even seed variable count. |
| Q-hessian | Hessian rank of the first polynomial | = | $16E$ | The positive coefficient sum in the finite product relation. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R-relation** [claim](#stmt-seed-product) / [proof](#proof-stmt-seed-product): Use prime valuations, exact finite-field rank, and a nonsingular integer minor. -> An integer product relation with positive coefficient sum.
- **R-chain** [claim](#stmt-seed-chain) / [proof](#proof-stmt-seed-chain): Complete each starting weight by decreasing integer remainders. -> An isolated chain with the prescribed starting weight.
- **R-seeds** [claim](#stmt-seed-seeds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-seed-seeds): Balance variable counts with squares and compare spectra by finite Fourier characters. -> Seeds with distinct orders and separate real-form and integral-module identifications.
- **R-prepared** [claim](#stmt-seed-prepared) / [proof](#proof-stmt-seed-prepared): Adjoin common pure powers with coprime degrees. -> Odd-order prepared data without a fixed eigenvalue.
- **R-symmetry** [claim](#stmt-local-symmetrize) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-symmetrize): Twist the Seifert form by an inverse of the doubling exponent. -> Reversible symmetric operator-form data.
- **R-local** [claim](#stmt-local-conclusion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-local-conclusion): Combine odd-prime tensor-square lifting, dyadic hermitian uniqueness, and rational local–global descent. -> Local integral and rational tensor-square isometries agreeing almost everywhere.
- **R-determinants** [claim](#stmt-global-determinants) / [proof](#proof-stmt-global-determinants): Tensor each map with its reverser conjugate and cancel determinants on all ordered eigenspace pairs. -> Unit determinant on each tensor eigenspace.
- **R-indefinite** [claim](#stmt-global-indefinite) / [proof](#proof-stmt-global-indefinite): Adjoin common cubes and the specified coprime pure powers. -> Indefinite hermitian eigenspaces without real eigenvalues.
- **R-integral** [claim](#stmt-global-integral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-global-integral): Apply strong approximation to the special unitary factors and intersect all local lattices. -> An integral equivariant isometry preserving Seifert data.
- **R-links** [claim](#stmt-topo-sphere) / [proof](#proof-stmt-topo-sphere): Use cyclotomic values, the Wang sequence, Alexander duality, and connectivity. -> Simple spherical links.
- **R-ambient** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#topo:ambient-map): Apply the spherical knot classification and extend along weighted radial coordinates. -> Ambiently homeomorphic germs with different multiplicities.
- **R-right** [claim](#stmt-topo-right-equivalence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-topo-right-equivalence): Transfer ambient equivalence to right equivalence and use real coefficients to remove conjugation. -> A right-equivalent pair with unequal multiplicities and parity.
- **R-initial** [claim](#stmt-topo-tangent-milnor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-topo-tangent-milnor): Add a common square and retain the full quadratic initial forms. -> Initial-form unit fibres with different zeroth homology.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-chain**: Completing a starting weight produces a finite isolated chain of weighted degree $1$, with positive weights less than $1/2$ and ordinary monomial degrees at least $3$.
- **I-orders**: Ambiently homeomorphic reduced hypersurface germs can have multiplicities $2$ and $3$.
- **I-seifert**: Equality of spectra alone does not identify the integral Seifert forms.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-spectrum**: Identify integral Seifert forms from equal spectra alone. -> The real-form and integral-monodromy identifications can be incompatible.
- **A-chain**: Apply the chain construction to arbitrary denominator and numerator inputs. -> The termination and terminal-degree argument uses the stated arithmetic hypotheses.
- **A-global**: Use a chain-routine test as a certificate of ambient equivalence. -> The passage to ambient germs requires integral forms and the spherical-knot classification.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The chain-completion lemma gives a finite decreasing-remainder construction; this harness covers that routine only.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON array of pairs [D,c], at most 1024 pairs.
# PRECONDITIONS: integers, D positive odd, 0<c<D/2; D<=3**12.
# OUTPUTS: JSON array of exponent/remainder chains or a rejected-input marker.
# TIME: O(D) exact divisions per pair; bit complexity depends on integer size.
# SPACE: O(D) integers per pair.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers and python-flint fmpq weights.
# FAILURE: inadmissible pairs yield {"error":"precondition"}; malformed JSON exits nonzero.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq
pairs = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
if not isinstance(pairs, list) or len(pairs)>1024:
    raise ValueError("batch")
result = []
for pair in pairs:
    if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair)!=2:
        result.append({"error":"precondition"})
        continue
    D, c = pair
    if type(D)!=int or type(c)!=int or not (0<D<=3**12 and D%2==1 and 0<2*c<D):
        result.append({"error":"precondition"})
        continue
    exponents, remainders, weights = [], [], []
    current = c
    for step in range(c):
        if current == 0:
            break
        remainders.append(current)
        weights.append(str(fmpq(current,D)))
        exponent = D // current
        exponents.append(exponent)
        current = D - exponent * current
    if current != 0:
        raise ArithmeticError("termination")
    result.append({"a":exponents,"c":remainders,"w":weights})
print(json.dumps(result,sort_keys=True))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-scope** (counterexample_scope): The counterexamples concern reduced real-coefficient weighted homogeneous polynomials with positive rational weights and isolated critical points in a common finite ambient dimension. Blocked use: ['Extending the existence statement to arbitrary nonreduced or nonisolated germs.']
- **L-dimension** (counterexample_scope): The construction supplies a finite dimension and does not claim a minimal ambient dimension. Blocked use: ['Claiming dimension optimality or a counterexample in every ambient dimension.']
- **L-metric** (counterexample_scope): The source homeomorphism is not claimed to be holomorphic or bi-Lipschitz. Blocked use: ['Using the pair as a counterexample under holomorphic or bi-Lipschitz equivalence.']
- **L-spectral** (hypothesis): Equality of spectra alone does not identify the integral Seifert forms. Blocked use: ['Replacing the integral isometry argument by spectral equality alone.']
- **L-separate** (edge_case): The seed identifications of real Seifert forms and integral monodromy modules are separate and are not asserted to be compatible. Blocked use: ['Treating the separate seed maps as a simultaneous integral Seifert isometry.']
- **L-dyadic** (hypothesis): Dyadic uniqueness assumes odd operator order and excludes eigenvalue $1$. Blocked use: ['Applying dyadic uniqueness to an arbitrary invariant symmetric form without the operator hypotheses.']
- **L-square-fixed** (edge_case): The tensor square can acquire eigenvalue $1$ even though the prepared operators have none; rational descent must retain the ordinary quadratic component. Blocked use: ['Omitting the eigenvalue-$1$ component from rational descent.']
- **L-approximation** (hypothesis): Strong approximation requires the group hypotheses and noncompactness of the archimedean product. Blocked use: ['Applying adelic density to arbitrary isometry groups or compact archimedean factors.']
- **L-global-finite** (nonconstructive): The integral isometry argument obtains a rational point by density in an adelic open coset; this passage alone does not specify a bounded search algorithm. Blocked use: ['Claiming an executed construction of the global lattice map from a finite chain routine.']
- **L-disjoint** (hypothesis): The polynomial sums use disjoint variable sets, so their gradient equations separate and monomials cannot cancel between copies. Blocked use: ['Reusing variables across summands while claiming the same isolation and order conclusions.']
- **L-parity** (edge_case): Multiplicity parity fails to be invariant for the pair, while the unequal Hessian coranks have the same parity. Blocked use: ['Claiming that the counterexample disproves Hessian-corank parity invariance.']
- **L-initial** (edge_case): Isolatedness concerns the stabilized germs rather than their initial forms; the full nonreduced initial form is retained. Blocked use: ['Replacing $t^2$ by $t$ or imposing isolatedness on the initial forms in this comparison.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a negative answer to the embedded Zariski multiplicity conjecture. We construct two reduced hypersurface germs that are ambiently homeomorphic but have multiplicities two and three. Both have isolated singularities and lie in a common complex affine space of dimension divisible by eight. Their defining function germs are also topologically right equivalent, and the same examples answer Arnold's corank problem negatively for ambient topological equivalence.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### The multiplicity problem

For a nonzero convergent power series $f\in\mathbb C\{z_1,\ldots,z_N\}$ with $f(0)=0$, the order $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0f$ is the least total degree of a monomial with nonzero coefficient. When $f$ is reduced, this integer is the multiplicity of the hypersurface germ $V(f)=\{f=0\}$. Two such germs are *ambiently homeomorphic* if a homeomorphism between neighborhoods of the origin in $\mathbb C^N$ fixes the origin and carries one hypersurface to the other. Zariski’s multiplicity question asks whether this embedded topological equivalence determines multiplicity (Zariski 1971) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Zariski1971](#ref-Zariski1971)]. It asks, in particular, whether topology can distinguish a hypersurface with a nonzero quadratic term from one whose first nonzero terms are cubic.

For an irreducible plane curve, the classical relation between embedded topology and the characteristic data of a branch determines its multiplicity (Zariski 1932) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Zariski1932](#ref-Zariski1932)]. Summing the branch multiplicities gives the same conclusion for a reduced plane curve. In every ambient dimension, the A’Campo–Lê theorem shows that a reduced hypersurface germ ambiently homeomorphic to a smooth one is itself smooth (A’Campo 1973; Lê Dũng Tráng 1973) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ACampo1973](#ref-ACampo1973), [Le1973](#ref-Le1973)]; see (Sampaio 2025, Theorem 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Sampaio2025Milnor](#ref-Sampaio2025Milnor)]. Thus multiplicity one cannot occur in an unequal-multiplicity pair. Eyral surveys the multiplicity problem and its further positive cases (Eyral 2007) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Eyral2007](#ref-Eyral2007)]. In higher dimensions, the Milnor fibration gives powerful topological invariants of an isolated singularity (Milnor 1968) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Milnor1968](#ref-Milnor1968)]. Its integral middle homology carries both a monodromy operator and a Seifert form. The latter records linking of cycles in neighboring fibers and, in a suitable high-dimensional range, classifies the embedded link. Multiplicity, by contrast, is read directly from the lowest-degree terms of an equation. Our construction separates these two kinds of information.

A polynomial is *weighted homogeneous with positive rational weights* if there are positive integers $a_1,\ldots,a_N,d$ such that $f(t^{a_1}z_1,\ldots,t^{a_N}z_N)=t^d f(z)$; the normalized weights are $w_i=a_i/d$. We prove the following existence statement.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a finite integer $N>3$, divisible by eight, and there are reduced polynomials $f_1,f_2\in\mathbb R[z_1,\ldots,z_N]$, weighted homogeneous for positive rational weights, such that:*

1.  *each $f_j$ has an isolated critical point at the origin;*

2.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0f_1=2$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0f_2=3$;*

3.  *there is a homeomorphism germ of ambient pairs $$(\mathbb C^N,V(f_1),0)\ \cong\ (\mathbb C^N,V(f_2),0).$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a negative answer to the embedded Zariski multiplicity conjecture. A single finite ambient dimension suffices for this purpose. The construction determines such a dimension by exact integer arithmetic; it is not intended to minimize it.

The same examples settle the corresponding questions for topological right equivalence. Holomorphic function germs $f,g:(\mathbb C^N,0)\to(\mathbb C,0)$ are *topologically right equivalent* if $f=g\circ\varphi$ for a homeomorphism germ $\varphi:(\mathbb C^N,0)\to(\mathbb C^N,0)$. The right-topological multiplicity conjecture asks whether reduced germs related in this way have equal multiplicities; its mod-two version asks only for equal multiplicity parity (Sampaio 2025, Conjecture 2 and Conjecture Z mod 2) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Sampaio2025Milnor](#ref-Sampaio2025Milnor)]. Corollary <a href="#topo:right-equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topo:right-equivalence">29</a> gives negative answers to both questions for the pair in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The source homeomorphism there may differ from the ambient map constructed below and is not claimed to be holomorphic or bi-Lipschitz.

A further consequence concerns Arnold’s corank problem. For a holomorphic germ $f:(\mathbb C^N,0)\to(\mathbb C,0)$ with a critical point at the origin, write $$\operatorname{corank}_0(f)
       =N-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{Hess}_0(f),$$ where $\operatorname{Hess}_0(f)$ is the Hessian matrix at the origin. Arnold’s question asks whether this number is preserved when a homeomorphism germ of $(\mathbb C^N,0)$ carries $V(f)$ to $V(g)$ for reduced holomorphic germs $f,g$ with critical points at the origin (Sampaio 2026, Problem 1). Corollary <a href="#topo:arnold-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topo:arnold-corank">28</a> gives a negative answer, even for the isolated weighted homogeneous germs with real coefficients in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

The examples also answer a question about the lowest-degree part of a function. For a nonzero holomorphic germ $g$ vanishing at the origin, let $g_*$ denote its lowest nonzero homogeneous component for ordinary total degree. Its *initial-form Milnor fibre* has the homotopy type of the global unit fibre $g_*^{-1}(1)$; the full polynomial $g_*$ is retained, even when it is nonreduced (Budur et al. 2022, Proposition 1.6 in arXiv v3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BudurEtAl2022ContactLoci](#ref-BudurEtAl2022ContactLoci)]. Motivated by the relationship between contact loci and monodromy, Budur, Fernández de Bobadilla, Lê, and Nguyen conjectured that these fibres are homotopy equivalent for embedded topologically equivalent function germs (Budur et al. 2022, Conjecture 1.7 in arXiv v3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BudurEtAl2022ContactLoci](#ref-BudurEtAl2022ContactLoci)]. Sampaio studies the explicit right-equivalence formulation (Sampaio 2025, Conjecture 4) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Sampaio2025Milnor](#ref-Sampaio2025Milnor)]. Corollary <a href="#topo:tangent-milnor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topo:tangent-milnor">30</a> disproves both formulations after adding one common square: the germs $f_j(z)+t^2$ in $\mathbb C^{N+1}$ remain topologically right equivalent and have isolated critical points, but their initial-form Milnor fibres are connected and have two connected components, respectively. Even their zeroth integral homology groups are therefore different.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Families, metric equivalence, and the present question

The question for an arbitrary pair must be distinguished from multiplicity in a family. Lê and Ramanujam showed that a one-parameter holomorphic deformation of isolated hypersurface singularities with constant Milnor number is topologically constant when the ambient complex dimension differs from three (Lê Dũng Tráng and Ramanujam 1976) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[LeRamanujam1976](#ref-LeRamanujam1976)]. In the excluded case $\mathbb C^3$, the companion (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[OpenAIMuConstant2026](#ref-OpenAIMuConstant2026)] proves, after shrinking, topological right-triviality for holomorphic one-parameter families $f_t$ with $f_t(0)=0$, an isolated critical point at the origin for every $t$, and constant $\mu(f_t,0)$. Greuel and O’Shea proved equimultiplicity for the corresponding deformation problem at an isolated quasihomogeneous singularity (Greuel 1986; O’Shea 1987) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Greuel1986](#ref-Greuel1986), [OShea1987](#ref-OShea1987)]. Fernández de Bobadilla and Pełka subsequently established equimultiplicity for continuous families with finite constant Milnor number, including coefficientwise continuous families of formal power series (Fernández de Bobadilla and Pełka 2024, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BobadillaPelka2022](#ref-BobadillaPelka2022)]. Their Example 8.6 gives topologically equivalent germs in $\mathbb C^4$, both of multiplicity two, that cannot be joined by a continuous $\mu$-constant family. They obtain the embedded equivalence by adding two independent squares to plane-curve germs with the same integral Seifert form, a precedent for the passage from integral forms to embedded topology used here. Thus an arbitrary equivalent pair does not reduce to the family problem. The two different multiplicities in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> therefore preclude a connecting $\mu$-constant family.

There are also positive results for pairs under additional geometric hypotheses. Yau established multiplicity invariance for pairs of isolated quasihomogeneous surface singularities in $\mathbb C^3$ (Yau 1988, Theorem C) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Yau1988](#ref-Yau1988)], crediting the detailed proofs jointly with Xu in the article’s added note. Metric and low-dimensional hypotheses give further positive results. Fernandes, Jelonek, and Sampaio proved that multiplicity two is preserved by ambient bi-Lipschitz equivalence, and that Hessian corank is preserved by ambient homeomorphisms in three complex variables (Fernandes, Jelonek, and Sampaio 2025, Corollary 3.5 and Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[FernandesJelonekSampaio2025](#ref-FernandesJelonekSampaio2025)]. The homeomorphism in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is topological and its ambient dimension is larger than three. Sampaio’s more recent corank-parity theorem gives an ambient topological invariant in every dimension (Sampaio 2026, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Sampaio2026](#ref-Sampaio2026)]. Corollary <a href="#topo:arnold-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topo:arnold-corank">28</a> is consistent with this theorem: the two Hessian ranks are even, so the unequal coranks have the same parity. These comparisons describe the precise topological setting of the counterexample.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The main ideas

The main difficulty is to preserve different ordinary orders while making the *integral Seifert forms* coincide. We compare the spectra, finite multisets of rational exponents whose exponentials give the monodromy eigenvalues. Equality of spectra alone does not accomplish the desired integral identification. For the isolated weighted homogeneous singularities considered here, in a fixed ambient dimension, the spectrum determines the real Seifert form, through the work of Steenbrink and Némethi (Steenbrink 1977, 1989; Némethi 1995; Balnojan and Hertling 2019) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Steenbrink1977](#ref-Steenbrink1977), [Steenbrink1989](#ref-Steenbrink1989), [Nemethi1995](#ref-Nemethi1995), [BalnojanHertling2019](#ref-BalnojanHertling2019)]. For sums of chain singularities, the integral-monodromy results of Hertling and Mase, building on Orlik and Randell, separately determine the integral module with its monodromy operator (Orlik 1972; Orlik and Randell 1977; Hertling and Mase 2022) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Orlik1972](#ref-Orlik1972), [OrlikRandell1977](#ref-OrlikRandell1977), [HertlingMase2022](#ref-HertlingMase2022)]. The two resulting identifications need not be compatible. The arithmetic part of the proof produces a single integral map preserving the whole Seifert form.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### 1. Seeds from a finite relation.

We begin with two multiplicative identities among explicitly specified positive rational numbers. A finite valuation matrix certifies an integer relation whose coefficient sum is positive. A decreasing integer-remainder construction completes each required weight to an isolated chain polynomial. The two collections of chains have different numbers of variables; balancing the difference by squares on one side produces multiplicity two there, while common cubes give multiplicity three on the other. The product identities and a triple-angle identity show that the spectra agree modulo two. The two classical classification results just described then supply a real Seifert-form isometry and an integral monodromy-module isomorphism (Section <a href="#seed:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="seed:section">3</a>).

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### 2. Compatible local and rational forms.

After adding common fifth and seventh powers, the monodromy has finite odd order and no eigenvalue one. In even ambient dimension, this permits an integral symmetrization of each Seifert form that retains the monodromy and recovers the original form. We prove that a first tensor square of these symmetric data has equivariant isometries over every $\mathbb Z_\ell$. At odd primes, square roots and sums of two squares in a finite operator algebra produce the maps. At two, the operator splits the lattice into dual pairs and unramified hermitian modules. A quadratic-form local–global argument then supplies an equivariant rational isometry (Section <a href="#local:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:section">4</a>).

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### 3. A single integral isometry.

The rational map and the local maps can still differ by nontrivial eigenspace determinants. Real coefficients provide integral involutions reversing monodromy. A second tensor square pairs each map with its conjugate by these involutions; an exact determinant calculation cancels the discrepancies on every eigenspace. Six common cubic variables make every relevant hermitian form indefinite. Two further common pure powers, of degrees eleven and thirteen, eliminate real eigenvalues. The determinant-one isometry group is now a product of special unitary groups. The strong-approximation method of Eichler, Kneser, and Platonov (Platonov 1969, 1970; Rapinchuk 2014) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Platonov1969](#ref-Platonov1969), [Platonov1970](#ref-Platonov1970), [Rapinchuk2014](#ref-Rapinchuk2014)] gives a rational group element meeting all prescribed local lattice conditions, and hence an integral Seifert-form isometry (Section <a href="#global:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:section">5</a>).

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### 4. From forms to ambient germs.

The Thom–Sebastiani theorem and Sakamoto’s integral Seifert-form formula realize the tensor operations by polynomial sums in disjoint variables (Sebastiani and Thom 1971; Sakamoto 1974) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[SebastianiThom1971](#ref-SebastianiThom1971), [Sakamoto1974](#ref-Sakamoto1974)]. The last two pure powers also force $\det(1-T)=1$ for the final monodromy $T$. The links are thus homotopy spheres, and the Milnor fibration verifies that they are simple knots. Levine’s classification converts the integral Seifert congruence into embedded link equivalence (Levine 1970) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Levine1970](#ref-Levine1970)]. Weighted radial coordinates extend that equivalence to a homeomorphism of ambient germs (Section <a href="#topo:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topo:section">6</a>). Figure <a href="#fig:proof-route" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-route">1</a> collects these stages and their distinct outputs.

Section <a href="#topo:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="topo:section">6</a> then applies the results of King and Saeki to obtain topological right equivalence. Real coefficients remove the complex-conjugation alternative in that transfer (King 1978; Saeki 1989; Sampaio 2025) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[King1978](#ref-King1978), [Saeki1989](#ref-Saeki1989), [Sampaio2025Milnor](#ref-Sampaio2025Milnor)]. Adding a square in one new variable preserves right equivalence and gives the different connectedness of the initial-form Milnor fibres.

Section <a href="#sec:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:background">2</a> fixes the conventions and exact classical inputs. Appendix <a href="#app:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate">7</a> contains the complete integer program for the finite rank certificate; a separately supplied exact relation can be verified by trial division alone. All construction, local algebra, determinant calculations, and the passage from local lattices to an integral map are proved in the text. The classical singularity-theoretic, arithmetic, and topological classification theorems are invoked after checking their hypotheses.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Conventions

Every sum combining singularities uses disjoint sets of variables. Superscript $[i]$ marks a fresh copy of a polynomial. An isometry of lattices with operators is required to intertwine those operators. The transpose of a matrix is $M^{\mathsf t}$. A lattice is a finite-rank free abelian group; a bilinear form is unimodular if its determinant is $\pm1$.

<a id="sec:background"></a>
### Classical inputs and conventions

This section records the classical results used below, together with their hypotheses and the conventions needed to apply them.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Milnor lattices, spectra, and integral monodromy

<a id="stmt-bg-milnor"></a>
<div id="bg:milnor" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Milnor). *Let $s\geq2$, and let $f:(\mathbb C^s,0)\to(\mathbb C,0)$ be a holomorphic germ with an isolated critical point at $0$, with $f(0)=0$. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, the link $K_f=f^{-1}(0)\cap S^{2s-1}_\varepsilon$ is a smooth closed manifold of dimension $2s-3$, and $$f/|f|:S^{2s-1}_\varepsilon\setminus K_f\longrightarrow S^1$$ is a locally trivial fibration. Each fiber is the interior of its closure $F_f$, a compact manifold with boundary $K_f$. Both $F_f$ and its interior are homotopy equivalent to a finite bouquet of $(s-1)$-spheres. The link is $(s-3)$-connected.*

</div>

<a id="eq:bg-monodromy"></a>
See (Milnor 1968, Theorems 4.8, 5.2, and 6.5, and Lemma 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Milnor1968](#ref-Milnor1968)]. The free group $L_f=\widetilde H_{s-1}(F_f;\mathbb Z)$ is the *Milnor lattice*; for a one-variable power we use the reduced zeroth homology of its finite Milnor fiber. The unimodular integral Seifert form is $S_f(x,y)=\operatorname{lk}(x,y^+)$, where the second cycle is pushed positively to a neighboring page. We use column vectors and fix the monodromy convention $$\label{eq:bg-monodromy}
 M_f=(-1)^sS_f^{-1}S_f^{\mathsf t}.$$ This amounts to using the inverse of the monodromy in the convention $S_f(Mx,y)=(-1)^sS_f(y,x)$; see (Balnojan and Hertling 2019, sec. 6, Equations (6.1)–(6.2)) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[BalnojanHertling2019](#ref-BalnojanHertling2019)]. Inverting monodromy throughout preserves every comparison made below. An isomorphism of Seifert forms means integral congruence unless a different coefficient field or ring is specified.

<a id="stmt-bg-ts"></a>
<div id="bg:ts" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Thom–Sebastiani–Sakamoto). *Suppose $f$ and $g$ have isolated critical points in $a$ and $b$ variables, respectively. There is an isomorphism of integral lattices $$L_{f+g}\cong L_f\otimes_{\mathbb Z}L_g$$ under which $$M_{f+g}\cong M_f\otimes M_g,
 \qquad
 S_{f+g}\cong(-1)^{ab}S_f\otimes S_g.$$ The assertion includes one-variable powers, with reduced zeroth homology.*

</div>

The tensor-product monodromy theorem originates with Sebastiani and Thom (Sebastiani and Thom 1971) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[SebastianiThom1971](#ref-SebastianiThom1971)]. The integral Seifert-form formula is (Sakamoto 1974, Theorem 2, p. 715) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Sakamoto1974](#ref-Sakamoto1974)]; the lattice and monodromy formulas are also recorded in (Balnojan and Hertling 2019, sec. 6, Equations (6.3)–(6.5)) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BalnojanHertling2019](#ref-BalnojanHertling2019)]. Thus comparisons of sums with the same factor dimensions have the same overall tensor sign.

A polynomial has *normalized positive weights* $w_1,\ldots,w_s\in\mathbb Q_{>0}$ if $f(\lambda^{w_1}z_1,\ldots,\lambda^{w_s}z_s)=\lambda f(z)$, understood after clearing denominators. We use positive spectral exponents $\beta\in(0,s)$, shifted by $1$ from the convention with spectrum in $(-1,s-1)$.

<a id="stmt-bg-spectrum"></a>
<div id="bg:spectrum" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:bg-spectrum"></a>
**Theorem 4** (Weighted homogeneous spectrum). *If $f$ is weighted homogeneous of normalized degree $1$ and has an isolated critical point, its spectral polynomial is $$\label{eq:bg-spectrum}
 P_f(t)=\sum_{\beta\in\operatorname{Sp}^{+}(f)}t^\beta
       =\prod_{j=1}^s\frac{t^{w_j}-t}{1-t^{w_j}}.$$ Exponents are counted with multiplicity. Their classes modulo $\mathbb Z$ determine the eigenvalues of $M_f$ by exponentiation, with a fixed choice of sign in the exponent. The operator $M_f$ has finite order.*

</div>

Here is the algebraic conversion from the usual spectrum formula to <a href="#eq:bg-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bg-spectrum">[eq:bg-spectrum]</a>. Steenbrink’s monomial formula (Steenbrink 1989, sec. 1, Examples, p. 165) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Steenbrink1989](#ref-Steenbrink1989)] assigns exponent $\sum_j(k_j+1)w_j$ to a monomial $z^k$ in a homogeneous basis of $\mathbb C[z_1,\ldots,z_s]/(\partial f)$. The derivatives have degrees $1-w_j$ and form a regular sequence: their common zero set is the origin, so the ideal has height $s$ in the Cohen–Macaulay polynomial ring (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 00ND and 02JN) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[StacksProject](#ref-StacksProject)]. The local regular-sequence criterion applies at the homogeneous maximal ideal; positive grading detects regularity there. Successive multiplication exact sequences therefore give $$\operatorname{Hilb}\bigl(\mathbb C[z]/(\partial f),t\bigr)
 =\prod_j\frac{1-t^{1-w_j}}{1-t^{w_j}}.$$ Multiplication by $t^{\sum_jw_j}$ yields <a href="#eq:bg-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bg-spectrum">[eq:bg-spectrum]</a>. Finally, the weighted coordinate rotation $z_j\mapsto e^{2\pi i w_j}z_j$ represents geometric monodromy; a common multiple of the weight denominators kills this rotation. Finite-order operators in characteristic zero are semisimple.

<a id="stmt-bg-real"></a>
<div id="bg:real" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Real Seifert forms from the spectrum). *Let $f$ and $g$ be isolated weighted homogeneous singularities in the same number of variables. If their multisets of positive spectral exponents agree modulo $2\mathbb Z$, their real Seifert forms are isomorphic.*

</div>

For clarity, this is a consequence of the spectral-pair classification, not an assertion about integral forms. Némethi’s theorem (Némethi 1995, Theorem 6.5) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Nemethi1995](#ref-Nemethi1995)] and (Balnojan and Hertling 2019, Theorem 4.4(b)) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[BalnojanHertling2019](#ref-BalnojanHertling2019)] say that spectral pairs modulo $2\mathbb Z$ in the first coordinate, together with the dimension, determine the real Seifert form. In the present semisimple case the nilpotent logarithm is zero. Put $m=s-1$. The actual cohomological weight is $m$ away from eigenvalue $1$ and $m+1$ at eigenvalue $1$; the definition of spectral pairs subtracts the latter shift, so every second coordinate is $m$. Thus this coordinate supplies no additional data. Translation $\beta\mapsto\beta-1$ respects equality modulo $2\mathbb Z$, and the normalization and passage from cohomology to homology depend only on $s$. More explicitly, the singularity construction gives a signed Steenbrink polarized mixed Hodge structure, whose defining replacement of the nilpotent logarithm by its negative leaves the data unchanged when that logarithm is zero (Balnojan and Hertling 2019, Definition 6.1 and Remark 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BalnojanHertling2019](#ref-BalnojanHertling2019)]. In that convention the normalized cohomological Seifert form is $(-1)^{(m+1)(m+2)/2}$ times the dual homological Seifert form (Balnojan and Hertling 2019, Equation (6.14)) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[BalnojanHertling2019](#ref-BalnojanHertling2019)]. The common sign and duality preserve isomorphism. This proves the stated consequence, including the eigenvalue-$1$ part.

<a id="stmt-bg-orlik"></a>
<div id="bg:orlik" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:bg-orlik"></a>
**Theorem 6** (Hertling–Mase). *Suppose $f$ is a sum of chains $$x_1^{a_1}x_2+\cdots+x_{r-1}^{a_{r-1}}x_r+x_r^{a_r},
 \qquad a_i\geq2,$$ including chains with $r=1$. Write the characteristic polynomial of $M_f$ as $\prod_d\Phi_d(t)^{c_d}$, where $\Phi_d$ is cyclotomic. Then, as an integral module with operator $t=M_f$, $$\label{eq:bg-orlik}
 (L_f,M_f)\cong
 \bigoplus_{j=1}^{\max c_d}\mathbb Z[t]/\bigl(p_j(t)\bigr),
 \qquad p_j(t)=\prod_{c_d\geq j}\Phi_d(t).$$ In particular, the eigenvalues with multiplicity determine this integral monodromy module within the indicated class of singularities.*

</div>

Orlik formulated the integral-monodromy decomposition (Orlik 1972) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Orlik1972](#ref-Orlik1972)]; the chain calculation of Orlik and Randell (Orlik and Randell 1977) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[OrlikRandell1977](#ref-OrlikRandell1977)] is a predecessor of the standard decomposition proved by Hertling and Mase. The precise result used here is (Hertling and Mase 2022, Theorem 1.3(a), (c), (d), and Definition 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[HertlingMase2022](#ref-HertlingMase2022)]. Reversing the variables puts our chains in that paper’s convention. Each standard block has an integral cyclic generator, hence is exactly the companion module displayed in <a href="#eq:bg-orlik" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bg-orlik">[eq:bg-orlik]</a>. Inverting its operator preserves cyclicity and its characteristic polynomial, whose roots are invariant under inversion. Thus <a href="#eq:bg-monodromy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bg-monodromy">[eq:bg-monodromy]</a> causes no change to the conclusion. This theorem controls the lattice with monodromy; it does not identify the integral Seifert form.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Arithmetic inputs

For a number field $F$, write $F_v$ for its completion at a place $v$.

<a id="stmt-bg-hasse"></a>
<div id="bg:hasse" class="theorem">

**Theorem 7** (Hasse–Minkowski). *Two nondegenerate quadratic forms over a number field $F$ are isometric over $F$ if and only if they are isometric over $F_v$ for every place $v$, including the archimedean places.*

</div>

We use the number-field form of the theorem; see (Milne 2022, Theorem 25.64(c) and the following discussion) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Milne2022](#ref-Milne2022)]; see also (O’Meara 1963, vol. 117, chap. VI) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[OMeara1963](#ref-OMeara1963)].

<a id="stmt-bg-approximation"></a>
<div id="bg:approximation" class="theorem">

**Theorem 8** (Strong approximation). *Let $G$ be a connected, simply connected, absolutely almost simple algebraic group over a number field $F$. If $\prod_{v\mid\infty}G(F_v)$ is noncompact, then $G(F)$ is dense in $G(\mathbb A_{F,f})$, the group of finite adelic points.*

</div>

See (Platonov 1969, Theorem A, p. 1140) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Platonov1969](#ref-Platonov1969)], with the correction in (Platonov 1970) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Platonov1970](#ref-Platonov1970)], and (Rapinchuk 2014, Theorem 2.3, p. 281) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Rapinchuk2014](#ref-Rapinchuk2014)]. Here the finite adelic group is the restricted product of the $G(F_v)$ over finite places, with respect to integral compact open subgroups. Consequently the density concerns approximation at finitely many places together with integrality at all remaining places. We apply the theorem to special unitary groups of nondegenerate hermitian forms over a totally imaginary quadratic extension of a totally real number field. In dimension at least two these groups are connected, simply connected, and absolutely almost simple; an indefinite archimedean component makes their archimedean product noncompact. The construction below verifies these hypotheses for every factor used.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Embedded high-dimensional topology

<a id="stmt-bg-classification"></a>
<div id="bg:classification" class="theorem">

**Theorem 9** (Smale and Levine). *A smooth closed manifold of dimension $d\geq5$ that is homotopy equivalent to $S^d$ is homeomorphic to $S^d$.*

*Let $q\geq3$, and let $K_1,K_2\subset S^{2q+1}$ be smooth oriented submanifolds homeomorphic to $S^{2q-1}$. Suppose the knots are *simple*, meaning $$\pi_1(S^{2q+1}\setminus K_j)\cong\mathbb Z,
 \qquad
 \pi_i(S^{2q+1}\setminus K_j)=0\quad(2\leq i\leq q-1).$$ If their integral Seifert matrices are congruent, then they are ambiently isotopic. In particular their embedded sphere pairs are homeomorphic.*

</div>

The first assertion is the high-dimensional generalized Poincaré theorem (Smale 1961, Theorem A, p. 391) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Smale1961](#ref-Smale1961)]. The second follows from (Levine 1970, Theorem 3, pp. 185–186) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Levine1970](#ref-Levine1970)]: integral congruence is a special case of the matrix equivalence in that theorem. Levine’s knots are required to be homeomorphic to spheres, so exotic smooth sphere structures are allowed. Related formulations for Milnor fibered links appear in (Durfee 1974) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Durfee1974](#ref-Durfee1974)] <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Saeki1999](#ref-Saeki1999)], (Saeki 1999, 698–99), and (Neumann 2003, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[Neumann2017](#ref-Neumann2017)]. The proof below checks the spherical and simplicity hypotheses directly before applying this theorem.

<a id="seed:section"></a>
### Two seeds with different orders

We first construct two sums of chains with the same spectrum modulo $2$, but with ordinary orders $2$ and $3$. The arithmetic ingredient is a finite relation between positive rational numbers.

<a id="stmt-seed-product"></a>
<div id="seed:product" class="lemma">

<a id="seed:products"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Finite product relation). *Set $d=3^{11}$ and $$\mathcal B=\{b\in\mathbb Z:0<b<d/2,\ 3\nmid b\},\qquad
 A_b=\frac{d-b}{b},\qquad
 R_b=\frac{(2d+b)(2d-b)}{(3d-b)(d+b)}.$$ There are integers $e_b$, $b\in\mathcal B$, such that $$\label{seed:products}
 E:=\sum_{b\in\mathcal B}e_b>0,
 \qquad \prod_{b\in\mathcal B}A_b^{e_b}
 =\prod_{b\in\mathcal B}R_b^{e_b}=1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-seed-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-seed-product</summary>

*Proof.* Form the integer matrix with column $b$ consisting of $(v_p(A_b))_p$ and $(v_p(R_b))_p$, with separate rows for the two families of prime valuations. It is enough to find an integer kernel vector whose coordinate sum is nonzero.

Here is the finite certificate for this assertion. Retain only the columns for which each of $$d-b,\ b,\ 2d+b,\ 2d-b,\ 3d-b,\ d+b$$ has no prime factor greater than $4450$. Repeatedly delete any column that is the only column supported on some nonzero row; discard zero rows. The resulting matrix $M$ and its augmentation $\widetilde M$ by a final row of ones satisfy

<div class="center">

|                        | columns | nonzero valuation rows |
|:-----------------------|--------:|-----------------------:|
| After the prime cutoff |  $1118$ |                 $1100$ |
| After all deletions    |   $896$ |                  $867$ |

</div>

<a id="seed:rank-certificate"></a>
and $$\label{seed:rank-certificate}
 \operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb F_{1009}}\widetilde M=868.$$ Appendix <a href="#app:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificate">7</a> gives a complete program, using only integer arithmetic and elimination modulo the prime $1009$, that verifies these counts and <a href="#seed:rank-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:rank-certificate">[seed:rank-certificate]</a>. Its sieve stores a prime divisor of every integer greater than one. Its six signed factor entries are precisely the two valuation columns above. In the elimination, a normalized pivot is stored for each leading row, so the number of stored pivots is the rank. Thus the certificate is a finite check of the displayed integer matrix, without numerical approximation.

Since $M$ has $867$ rows, <a href="#seed:rank-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:rank-certificate">[seed:rank-certificate]</a> gives an $868$-column square submatrix $H$ of $\widetilde M$ whose integer determinant is nonzero. The vector $$z=\operatorname{adj}(H)(0,\ldots,0,1)^{\mathsf t}$$ is integral, satisfies the valuation equations on these columns, and has coordinate sum $\det H$. Extend $z$ by zeros to all columns $b\in\mathcal B$, and change its overall sign if necessary. This gives $e_b$ with $E=|\det H|>0$. Every omitted valuation row is zero on the retained columns, so all prime valuations of both products in <a href="#seed:products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:products">[seed:products]</a> vanish. A positive rational number with this property is $1$, proving the Lemma. ◻

</details>

The adjugate prescription also makes the construction entirely finite: one may select the lexicographically first nonsingular minor, with columns ordered by $b$ and valuation rows ordered by family and prime. No bound on the size of its coefficients is needed.

<a id="stmt-seed-chain"></a>
<div id="seed:chain" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Completing one weight to a chain). *Let $D$ be a positive odd integer and let $c\in\mathbb Z$ satisfy $0<c<D/2$. There is a chain polynomial <a id="seed:chain-polynomial"></a>$$\label{seed:chain-polynomial}
 F_c=x_1^{a_1}x_2+\cdots+x_{\ell-1}^{a_{\ell-1}}x_\ell
       +x_\ell^{a_\ell}$$ of weighted degree $1$, whose first weight is $c/D$, all of whose weights are positive and less than $1/2$, and all of whose ordinary monomial degrees are at least $3$. It has an isolated critical point at the origin. Removing its first variable and first monomial gives a chain with exactly the remaining weights, when $\ell>1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-seed-chain"><summary>Proof of stmt-seed-chain</summary>

*Proof.* Set $c_1=c$ and successively perform the divisions $$a_i=\lfloor D/c_i\rfloor,\qquad c_{i+1}=D-a_ic_i,$$ stopping at the first zero remainder. Each nonzero remainder is a positive integer strictly smaller than its predecessor, so this procedure terminates. Give $x_i$ weight $c_i/D$. Then $a_ic_i+c_{i+1}=D$ for each nonterminal term, and $a_\ell c_\ell=D$ for the terminal term. All $a_i\ge2$. Consequently each nonterminal monomial has ordinary degree $a_i+1\ge3$. The terminal exponent divides the odd integer $D$ and exceeds $2$, so it too is at least $3$.

To prove isolation, suppose a nonzero point is critical and let $j$ be the first index with $x_j\ne0$. If $j=\ell$, the derivative in $x_j$ is $a_\ell x_\ell^{a_\ell-1}\ne0$. Otherwise that derivative forces $x_{j+1}=0$, because the preceding coordinate is zero. The derivative in $x_{j+1}$ is then $x_j^{a_j}\ne0$: its other term vanishes since $a_{j+1}\ge2$. Both cases contradict criticality. The same reasoning applies to the tail, which has the stated weights. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-seed-seeds"></a>
<div id="seed:seeds" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12** (The seeds). *There are real-coefficient weighted homogeneous polynomials $p_1,p_2$, each in an even number $s_0$ of variables, such that:*

1.  *both are sums, in disjoint variables, of chains with exponents at least $2$, and both have isolated critical points;*

2.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0p_1=2$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_0p_2=3$;*

3.  *their spectral multisets are equal modulo $2$;*

4.  *their real Seifert forms are isomorphic, and, separately, their integral monodromy modules are isomorphic;*

5.  *both monodromy operators have order dividing $D=3^{12}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-seed-seeds"><summary>Proof of stmt-seed-seeds</summary>

<a id="seed:weight-table"></a>
*Proof.* Fix the integers in Lemma <a href="#seed:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="seed:product">10</a>, and put $D=3d$. The four weights below are chosen for the triple-angle identity in the spectral comparison; their signed counts will give side $2$ an excess of $4E$ variables, to be balanced by squares on side $1$. Start with two lists of weights prescribed by the following signed counts; a positive entry means that many variables on side $2$, and a negative entry means its absolute value on side $1$: $$\label{seed:weight-table}
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
 w& b/d&b/(3d)&(d-b)/(3d)&(d+b)/(3d)\\\hline
 \text{count on side $2$ minus count on side $1$}
   &2e_b&-2e_b&2e_b&2e_b.
\end{array}$$ All these weights are positive and less than $1/2$. Their numerators with denominator $D$ are $3b,b,d-b,d+b$, respectively.

Realize each occurrence of a starting weight $c/D$ by its own copy of the chain in Lemma <a href="#seed:chain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="seed:chain">11</a>. On the opposite side insert an independent copy of the tail of that chain, doing nothing there if the tail is empty. The added tail weights occur equally on both sides. All variables used for different copies are disjoint. Thus this operation realizes the signed weight counts exactly by sums of isolated chains, and all its monomials have degree at least $3$.

The variable excess on side $2$ is $4E$. Add $4E$ independent squares to side $1$, giving both sides the same number of variables. Every starting multiplicity in <a href="#seed:weight-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:weight-table">[seed:weight-table]</a> is even, as is the number of copies of each tail, and $4E$ is even. Both variable counts are therefore even already. Finally add two independent pure cubes to each side, and call the resulting polynomials $p_1,p_2$. They have the same even number $s_0$ of variables. Sums in disjoint variables have critical loci equal to products of the individual critical loci, so both critical points are isolated. All coefficients can be taken to be $1$. Squares occur only in $p_1$, and the common cubes occur in both polynomials, proving the asserted ordinary orders.

<a id="seed:spectrum-product"></a>
We next compare the spectra. In the convention of Theorem <a href="#bg:spectrum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bg:spectrum">4</a>, write $$\label{seed:spectrum-product}
 P_p(t)=\sum_{\alpha\in\operatorname{Sp}^{+}(p)}t^\alpha
        =\prod_{w\text{ a weight of }p}\frac{t^w-t}{1-t^w}.$$ All exponents lie in $(2D)^{-1}\mathbb Z$. Reduction modulo $2$ puts their multiplicities in the group algebra of the finite cyclic group $(2D)^{-1}\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$. Its characters are $\alpha\mapsto e^{\pi i k\alpha}$, for integer $k$. By Fourier inversion, it suffices to prove that $P_{p_1}$ and $P_{p_2}$ take the same value at every such character. When an individual quotient in <a href="#seed:spectrum-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:spectrum-product">[seed:spectrum-product]</a> is indeterminate, evaluate along $t^u=\exp((\pi i k+\varepsilon)u)$ and let $\varepsilon\to0$.

<a id="seed:even-factor"></a>
*Case 1: $k=2m$ is even.* For a weight $w$, its individual factor has value $$\label{seed:even-factor}
 \left.\frac{t^w-t}{1-t^w}\right|_k
 =\begin{cases}
 -1,&mw\notin\mathbb Z,\\
 (1-w)/w,&mw\in\mathbb Z.
 \end{cases}$$ The second value follows by taking the ratio of the two first derivatives in $\varepsilon$. All these values are nonzero, so common weights can be canceled.

The first column of <a href="#seed:weight-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:weight-table">[seed:weight-table]</a> has exact reduced denominator $d$, and the other three have exact reduced denominator $3d$, since $3\nmid b$. Within either denominator group, therefore, all factors are exceptional in <a href="#seed:even-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:even-factor">[seed:even-factor]</a>, or none are. The exceptional signed product for the first group is $\prod_b A_b^{2e_b}=1$. For the other group it is $$\prod_b\left(
 \frac{b}{3d-b}\frac{2d+b}{d-b}\frac{2d-b}{d+b}
 \right)^{2e_b}
 =\prod_b(R_b/A_b)^{2e_b}=1.$$ The nonexceptional signs disappear because all signed counts are even. A square has exceptional value $1$ and nonexceptional value $-1$; its total contribution is $1$, since there are $4E$ squares. This proves equality in Case 1, including $k=0$.

*Case 2: $k$ is odd and $3\nmid k$.* Each factor is $$\left.\frac{t^w-t}{1-t^w}\right|_k
     =i\cot(\pi k w/2).$$ All chain weights have a reduced denominator that is a nontrivial power of $3$. None of their cotangents has a zero or pole in this case: either would imply that this denominator divides $k$. Squares also have finite nonzero factors. Hence common weights may again be canceled.

<a id="seed:cotangent"></a>
Put $C(u)=\cot(\pi ku/2)$. The exact signed identity we need is $$\label{seed:cotangent}
 C(b/d)=
 \frac{C(b/(3d))}{C((d-b)/(3d))C((d+b)/(3d))}.$$ Indeed, with $x=\pi kb/(6d)$ the usual triple-angle product gives $$\cot x\,\cot(x+k\pi/3)\,\cot(x+2k\pi/3)=-\cot(3x).$$ The shifts by $k\pi/3$ give the same three factors modulo $\pi$ as the shifts by $\pi/3$, because $3\nmid k$. Since $k$ is odd, $$\cot(x+k\pi/3)=\frac{1}{C((d-b)/(3d))},\qquad
 \cot(x+2k\pi/3)=-\frac{1}{C((d+b)/(3d))}.$$ Substitution proves <a href="#seed:cotangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:cotangent">[seed:cotangent]</a>. Its $2e_b$-th power is exactly the assertion that the signed cotangent product in row $b$ of <a href="#seed:weight-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:weight-table">[seed:weight-table]</a> equals $1$. Each square contributes $C(1/2)=\cot(\pi k/4)\in\{1,-1\}$, whose $4E$-th power is $1$. Finally the powers of $i$ are the same because the two complete polynomials have the same number $s_0$ of variables. This proves equality in Case 2.

*Case 3: $k$ is odd and $3\mid k$.* A pure cube has spectrum $t^{1/3}+t^{2/3}$, whose value is $-1+1=0$. Before the final common cubes were inserted, both sides were already isolated sums of chains and squares, so their spectra were finite polynomials. Multiplication by a cube spectrum therefore makes both character values zero. This argument uses those finite polynomials, and does not cancel a zero against a pole of an individual weight quotient. It completes the Fourier comparison.

Weighted homogeneous monodromy has finite order and hence is semisimple. Theorem <a href="#bg:real" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bg:real">5</a> now converts the equality of spectra modulo $2$ in the common dimension into an isomorphism of real Seifert forms. The same equality gives equal eigenvalue multiplicities. All summands are chains, including the pure powers, so Theorem <a href="#bg:orlik" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bg:orlik">6</a> gives an isomorphism of integral monodromy modules. These are separate isomorphisms; no compatibility between them is asserted here.

Finally, every nonsquare weight has denominator dividing $D$. Each square has spectrum $t^{1/2}$, so the $4E$ squares together contribute $t^{2E}$. All spectral exponents of either seed therefore belong to $D^{-1}\mathbb Z$. Their monodromy eigenvalues are $D$-th roots of unity. Semisimplicity then gives $M_{p_j}^D=1$ for $j=1,2$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-seed-prepared"></a>
<div id="seed:prepared" class="corollary">

**Corollary 13** (Removing eigenvalue one). *In two new variables on each side, put <a id="seed:q-definition"></a>$$\label{seed:q-definition}
 q_j=p_j+x^5+y^7,\qquad j=1,2,
 \qquad s=s_0+2.$$ These are real-coefficient isolated sums of chains in the same even number $s$ of variables, with orders $2$ and $3$. Their real Seifert forms are isomorphic, and their integral monodromy modules are isomorphic. Both monodromies have odd order dividing $$m=3^{12}\cdot5\cdot7,$$ and neither has eigenvalue $1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-seed-prepared"><summary>Proof of stmt-seed-prepared</summary>

*Proof.* The new terms preserve isolation and ordinary orders. Formula <a href="#seed:spectrum-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="seed:spectrum-product">[seed:spectrum-product]</a> multiplies each seed spectrum by the same two pure-power spectra, preserving equality modulo $2$. Theorems <a href="#bg:real" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bg:real">5</a> and <a href="#bg:orlik" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bg:orlik">6</a> consequently apply again. Each new eigenvalue has the form $$\alpha\beta\gamma,\qquad
 \alpha^D=1,\quad
 \beta^5=\gamma^7=1,\quad \beta\ne1,\quad\gamma\ne1.$$ Because $D$, $5$, and $7$ are pairwise coprime, its order divides $35D$ and is divisible by both $5$ and $7$. In particular it is not $1$. Finite-order weighted homogeneous monodromy is semisimple, so the same bound holds for the order of the operators themselves. ◻

</details>

<a id="local:section"></a>
### Local and rational isometries of tensor squares

Let $q_1,q_2$ be the polynomials of Corollary <a href="#seed:prepared" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="seed:prepared">13</a>, in the same even number $s$ of variables. Write $L_j$ for their Milnor lattices and $S_j$ for their integral Seifert forms. The two inputs from that corollary are an isomorphism of the integral monodromy modules and an isomorphism of the real Seifert forms. We now combine these inputs, first locally and then rationally.

Choose an odd integer $m$ divisible by the orders of both monodromies, and choose $r\in\mathbb Z$ with $2r\equiv1\pmod m$. With $s$ even and the monodromy convention in Equation <a href="#eq:bg-monodromy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bg-monodromy">[eq:bg-monodromy]</a>, put <a id="local:symmetric-data"></a>$$\label{local:symmetric-data}
 T_j=S_j^{-1}S_j^{\mathsf t},\qquad B_j=S_jT_j^r.$$ Throughout, an *equivariant isometry* between data $(L,T,B)$ means an isometry of the forms that intertwines the displayed operators. Tensor products carry both the tensor product form and the tensor product operator.

<a id="stmt-local-symmetrize"></a>
<div id="local:symmetrize" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Symmetrization). *Each $B_j$ is an integral unimodular symmetric form preserved by $T_j$. The pair $(B_j,T_j)$ recovers $S_j$ by $S_j=B_jT_j^{-r}$.
